138	\midrule
139	Base model & Qwen3-1.7B & Qwen3-1.7B \\
140	Architecture & Linear + LoRA & SFT + LoRA \\
141	LoRA $r$ / $\alpha$ & 16 / 32 & 16 / 32 \\
142	Learning rate & 2e-5 & 2e-4 \\
143	Optimizer & AdamW & AdamW \\
144	Epochs & 3 & 3 \\
145	Batch size (per device) & 4 & 4 \\
146	Gradient accumulation & 8 & 8 \\
147	Warmup ratio & 0.1 & 0.1 \\
148	Weight decay & 0.01 & 0.01 \\
149	Max sequence length & 512 & 512 \\
150	Seeds & \{42\} & \{42, 123, 456, 789\} \\
151	\bottomrule
152	\end{tabular}}
153	\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Both architectures use the same base model with LoRA adaptation. The classification head degenerates across all configurations; the implicit router results are reported as 4-seed mean$\pm$std.}
154	\label{tab:hyperparams}
155	\end{table}
156	
157	\section{Seed Robustness}
158	\label{app:seed-robustness}
159	
160	We assess router stability under two conditions: (1) \emph{fixed split}---same 81/14 train/validation partition, only the training seed varies (seeds $\{42, 123, 456, 789\}$); and (2) \emph{cross-split}---both the partition and the training seed vary (seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45\}$). Table~\ref{tab:seed-robustness} reports per-seed results.

The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.

A preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), but full-scale multi-model validation remains future work. The $-100$ death penalty may amplify DAF; environments with less catastrophic penalties could exhibit lower DAF, though the qualitative finding may still hold. On the 135 episodes (55.1\\%) where neither strategy leads to catastrophic failure, deterministic computation retains a small advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$), indicating non-zero routing signal beyond death avoidance. Our analysis covers 11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types; the remaining 19 (63\\%) were excluded during prescreening because both strategies timed out on all seed variations within the 30-step budget, providing no counterfactual signal. These tasks likely require longer-horizon planning; increasing the step budget could surface additional routing signal whose composition may differ from the death-dominated pattern observed here. Stronger models (e.g., 70B-scale or reasoning-specialized) likely exhibit lower internal-strategy death rates, which would reduce DAF. Our finding may reflect a capability-dependent failure mode: at higher capability levels, death avoidance may no longer dominate routing signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment scope.}\nOur MATH and ALFWorld analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide cross-environment evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. In MATH (no death penalty), the deterministic advantage vanishes with a marginally significant reversal favoring internal reasoning ($+4.3$\\,pp, $p = 0.079$, not reaching $\\alpha{=}0.05$). In ALFWorld, the deterministic advantage stems from a capability gap rather than death avoidance. Environments with graded failure costs or intermediate penalty regimes remain unstudied.\n\n\\paragraph{Reasoning strategy scope.}\nOur internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct \\citep{react-2023}, which lacks self-correction or backtracking. More sophisticated reasoning strategies---such as Reflexion-style self-correction \\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking \\citep{tot-2023}---may reduce the internal strategy's death rate, which would lower DAF. Our findings may therefore be partly specific to the vulnerability of single-pass reasoning rather than a universal property of internal computation.\n\n\\paragraph{Step-level mislabeling.} Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing decisions to individual steps; in particular, steps preceding a death event are labeled det-preferred even if the death was not foreseeable from those steps' observations. Training on pure-strategy trajectories but deploying on mixed policy also introduces a distribution shift \\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nExtending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level labeling with denser rewards and the logit--generation gap in the implicit router are natural next steps. We audit labeling quality in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference} labels (61.2\\%), 115 (46.9\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\%) are both-dead---all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields balanced accuracy of 57.3\\%, within 1$\\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\\pm$ 0.5\\%), confirming that score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. ```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Within completable-horizon tasks (11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry alone---a penalty-independent measure. The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor reaches ${\sim}90\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, with per-task correlation $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) between death-rate differential and performance gap; we note that this correlation is partly structural, as death penalties contribute directly to score differences (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). A trained router achieves only 57--59\% balanced accuracy, consistent with extreme class imbalance induced by death dominance. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows routing signal vanishes without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a marginally significant reversal of $+4.3$ percentage points ($p = 0.079$). A Llama-3.1-8B pilot provides preliminary directional evidence that death-driven routing generalizes across model families. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the router functions primarily as a safety critic rather than an efficiency optimizer.
3	\end{abstract}
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet understanding \\emph{what} drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Within completable-horizon tasks (11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry alone---a penalty-independent measure. The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor reaches ${\\sim}90\\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, with per-task correlation $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) between death-rate differential and performance gap; we note that this correlation is partly structural, as death penalties contribute directly to score differences (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). A trained router achieves only 57--59\\% balanced accuracy, consistent with extreme class imbalance induced by death dominance. Cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$) shows routing signal vanishes without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a marginally significant reversal of $+4.3$ percentage points ($p = 0.079$). A Llama-3.1-8B pilot provides preliminary directional evidence that death-driven routing generalizes across model families. These findings indicate that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the router functions primarily as a safety critic rather than an efficiency optimizer.\n\\end{abstract}",
      "numLines": 3,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 3
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}
5	The death-avoidance finding in ScienceWorld raises a natural question: does routing signal persist in environments without catastrophic failure? An evaluation on MATH Level~4--5 (300 problems: 152 Level~4, 148 Level~5), using code interpreter as the deterministic modality, suggests not. Internal reasoning directionally outperforms code interpreter: 81.0\% accuracy (95\% CI [76.7, 85.3]) versus 76.7\% (95\% CI [72.0, 81.3]); the paired difference of $+4.3$~percentage points (95\% CI [0.0, $+8.7$]; McNemar $p = 0.079$) is marginally significant but does not reach the conventional $\alpha{=}0.05$ threshold. The label distribution inverts relative to ScienceWorld: 30 internal-preferred versus 17 deterministic-preferred (compared to 9:86 in ScienceWorld), with 84.3\% of problems (253 of 300) showing no modality preference. In the absence of death risk, the routing signal vanishes or reverses---consistent with death avoidance as the primary driver of the deterministic strategy's advantage observed in ScienceWorld. Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal} and Table~\ref{tab:cross-env} summarize this label distribution inversion.
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
10	\caption{Label distribution reversal across environments. In ScienceWorld (with $-100$ death penalty), deterministic-preferred labels dominate (86:9). In MATH (no death penalty), the pattern inverts (17:30), with 84.3\% showing no preference.}
11	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\small
17	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
18	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
19	\toprule
20	Environment & $N$ & Det\% & Int\% & NoPref\% & Death? \\
21	\midrule
22	ScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\
23	MATH & 300 & 5.7 & 10.0 & 84.3 & No \\
24	ALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} \\
25	\bottomrule
26	\end{tabular}}
27	\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. Det/Int/NoPref: percentage of episodes labeled deterministic-preferred, internal-preferred, and no-preference. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from action-space pruning (capability gap), not death avoidance.}
28	\label{tab:cross-env}
29	\end{table}
30	
31	\paragraph{Implications for router design.}
32	The dominance of death avoidance in routing signal (DAF ${\sim}90\%$ at default $P{=}100$) reframes what a modality router is learning. Rather than discovering efficiency-quality tradeoffs across reasoning strategies, the router primarily learns to predict which states lead to catastrophic failure under internal reasoning. This aligns the routing problem more closely with \emph{safety critics} in safe reinforcement learning~\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} than with the cost-quality optimization framing adopted by existing routing work~\citep{cogrouter-2026, ares-2026, mtrouter-2026}. The practical implication is direct: if death avoidance accounts for the vast majority of routing value, then a lightweight death predictor---trained on state features correlated with hallucination-induced failure---may capture most of the router's benefit without the overhead of full counterfactual labeling. That the oracle outperforms always-deterministic by only 2.89 points further suggests that the residual, non-death routing signal may not justify the complexity of learning a full routing policy.
33	
34	\paragraph{Label imbalance as a core finding.}
35	The extreme label distribution---86 deterministic-preferred, 9 internal-preferred, and 150 no-preference episodes---is not an artifact to be corrected but a central result. The 61.2\% no-preference rate indicates that for most episodes, modality choice is irrelevant: both strategies succeed or both fail. Among episodes where modality matters, deterministic is preferred at a 9.6:1 ratio, driven almost entirely by death asymmetry. This explains why always-deterministic is a strong baseline: the router's marginal value is confined to identifying the ${\sim}$4\% of episodes where internal reasoning is genuinely superior. The near-random balanced accuracy of trained routers (57--59\%) is an illustrative negative result that reinforces the death-dominance finding: the extreme class imbalance induced by death asymmetry (86:9 det:int) leaves insufficient minority-class signal for supervised learning to exploit, structurally constraining any router trained on these labels. Recent concurrent work on tool-use calibration reaches compatible conclusions from the opposite direction---that LLMs may already possess implicit routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026} but that external tool invocation carries non-trivial costs~\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, reinforcing the case for conservative routing defaults.
36	
37	\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity and generalization.}
38	The DAF's sensitivity to penalty magnitude---ranging from 46\% at $P{=}10$ to 95\% at $P{=}200$---highlights that death-avoidance dominance is not an invariant property of routing but a function of the environment's penalty structure. In domains with mild failure costs, non-death routing signals (e.g., reasoning quality differences on surviving episodes) would constitute a larger share of total routing value. This connects to reward shaping more broadly: the relative magnitude of catastrophic versus incremental penalties shapes which behavioral differences a router can exploit.
39	
40	Our findings may also be capability-dependent. The 42.9\% internal-reasoning death rate reflects the failure mode of a 7B-parameter model prone to state hallucination. Larger models with lower hallucination rates would exhibit smaller death-rate differentials, potentially reducing DAF and revealing routing signal currently masked by catastrophic failures. Whether non-death routing signal \emph{emerges} as model capability increases---or remains negligible---is an open question with direct implications for the modality routing agenda.
41	
42	\paragraph{Scope and future directions.}
43	Our analysis is grounded in a single interactive environment with a specific failure mode (the $-100$ death penalty). Extending the counterfactual methodology to environments with graded failure costs, embodied agents with continuous action spaces, or multi-turn dialogue systems would clarify how broadly death avoidance---or analogous catastrophic-failure avoidance---accounts for apparent routing signal. Step-level counterfactual labeling, rather than episode-level, is a complementary direction that could reveal finer-grained routing structure masked by our current aggregation. A further limitation is the gap between the implicit routing signal we measure---based on episode outcomes---and the logit-level representations an implicit router would use at inference time; bridging this gap requires probing whether outcome-level modality preferences are recoverable from the model's internal activations before action selection. Finally, validating these findings across model scales and families beyond 7--8B parameters would establish whether the death-avoidance dominance we observe is a property of the environment or of the capability regime.
44	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
10	But this assumption has never been systematically verified: \emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}
11	
12	We find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \textbf{death avoidance accounts for ${\sim}$90\% of the routing signal} (at default penalty $P{=}100$) in ScienceWorld.
13	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
14	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.
15	
16	Reaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.
17	The standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.
18	In ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
19	Keyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\S\ref{sec:baselines}).
20	We instead propose \emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
21	
22	Across 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.
23	Excluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; roughly four-fifths of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.
24	The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \S\ref{sec:daf}) is ${\sim}90\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\% ($P{=}200$).
25	The per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
26	
27	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
28	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a marginally significant reversal ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$): internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.
29	In the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.
30	Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.
31	As a downstream consequence, a trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only ${\sim}58\%$ (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	Our contributions are:
34	\begin{enumerate}
35	    \item \textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}
36	    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}
39	    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude. The penalty-sensitive DAF reaches ${\sim}90\%$ at the default $P{=}100$ but is lower at milder penalties (46\% at $P{=}10$), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
40	
41	    \item \textbf{Cross-context validation.}
42	    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), providing evidence that the finding is driven by failure structure rather than an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.
43	\end{enumerate}
44	
45	The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
46	\S\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.
47	\S\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.
48	\S\ref{sec:baselines}--\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.
49	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.
50	
```

### Tool result

_source line 2212 · 2026-05-15T16:05:44.583Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UX3kSqz6FcdojZmjm2WGrr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced episode-level counterfactual analysis as a methodology for decomposing the routing signal in modular LLM agents into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes within completable-horizon tasks (11 of 30 task types) with a Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone, this analysis reveals that roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry alone---a penalty-independent measure. The penalty-sensitive DAF reaches ${\sim}90\%$ at the default $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_\text{full} = 29.60$ vs.\ $\Delta_\text{nd} = 2.84$), with strong cross-task correlation ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$).
4	
5	Two lines of evidence support the generality of this finding. First, cross-environment analysis on MATH ($N{=}300$), where no death penalty exists, shows that the routing signal vanishes and directionally reverses: internal reasoning directionally outperforms code execution by $+4.3$ percentage points (marginally significant, $p = 0.079$; \S\ref{sec:discussion}), and the label distribution inverts from 86:9 deterministic-preferred in ScienceWorld to 30:17 internal-preferred. This contrast confirms that death avoidance, not an inherent advantage of deterministic computation, is the primary driver. Second, a Llama-3.1-8B pilot ($N{=}10$, insufficient for statistical inference) replicates the direction of the death differential, providing directional but not conclusive evidence of cross-model consistency.
6	
7	As a downstream consequence, a trained routing classifier achieves only 57--59\% balanced accuracy across four seeds---an illustrative negative result reinforcing the death-dominance finding: the extreme class imbalance induced by death asymmetry structurally constrains supervised routers trained on these labels (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
8	
9	These results carry a practical implication: in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety rather than efficiency. The router functions primarily as a safety critic---steering away from fatal actions---rather than as an optimizer selecting the more efficient strategy. System designers may therefore benefit from treating routing as a risk-management mechanism in such settings.
10	
11	Several directions remain open. Step-level counterfactual labeling could localize where within an episode routing decisions matter most. Stronger backbone models with lower intrinsic death rates may yield lower DAF values, revealing whether non-death routing signal emerges as the safety floor rises. Finally, environments with graded failure costs (rather than binary death) would test whether the dominance of catastrophic-failure avoidance extends to softer penalty structures.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QaK1cRwVq3sBFZfgsyL9Jc`</summary>

```text
12	
13	The full performance gap between deterministic and internal strategies across 245 episodes is $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$ points. When we exclude all episodes where either strategy results in agent death, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. The resulting Death Avoidance Factor (\S\ref{sec:daf}) is ${\sim}90\%$ (at the default penalty $P{=}100$): approximately nine-tenths of the deterministic strategy's advantage over internal reasoning is attributable to death avoidance rather than superior task performance when both agents survive.
14	
15	This concentration is consistent across task types (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). The Pearson correlation between per-task death rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is $r = 0.9698$ ($p = 8.12 \times 10^{-7}$; bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$), as shown in Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}. Spearman rank correlation is $\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \times 10^{-8}$), and leave-one-out analysis shows $r \in [0.963, 0.980]$---no single task type is a leverage point (Appendix~\ref{app:correlation}). We note that death rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ are structurally coupled through the penalty term $P$, so high correlation is expected when death dominates. The empirically non-trivial finding is that the large majority of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry independent of $P$.\footnote{Exact fractions depend on edge-case adjudication; we report the precise breakdown in \S\ref{sec:labeling} and Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.} Knowing how much more frequently the internal strategy dies is nearly sufficient to predict the full performance gap for a given task type. The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes compared to 16.3\% for deterministic computation---a 2.6$\times$ ratio that drives the bulk of the observed score difference.
16	
17	The episode-level label distribution reflects this asymmetry: of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, only 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}---when internal reasoning does not cause death, it performs comparably to deterministic computation.
18	
19	Notably, the per-task DAF distribution is bimodal: \texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits DAF $\approx 0\%$ (routing signal entirely from non-death score differences, $\Delta = +9.58$), while all other task types exceed 85\%.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016tfz44Cohhs7AjRgCTiAFX`</summary>

```text
80	\end{algorithm}
81	
82	The four mutually exclusive cases correspond to:
83	\begin{enumerate}
84	    \item \textbf{Death asymmetry} (lines 5--8): $d^{\tt int} \neq d^{\tt det}$ $\rightarrow$ the surviving strategy is preferred. This accounts for the large majority of all non-tie labels.
85	    \item \textbf{Both dead} (lines 9--10): $d^{\tt int} = d^{\tt det} = 1$ $\rightarrow$ ${\tt no\_preference}$.
86	    \item \textbf{Score comparison} (lines 11--14): both survive and $s^{\tt det} \neq s^{\tt int}$ $\rightarrow$ the higher-scoring strategy is preferred.
87	    \item \textbf{Tied alive} (lines 15--16): both survive and $s^{\tt det} = s^{\tt int}$ $\rightarrow$ ${\tt no\_preference}$.
88	\end{enumerate}
89	
90	Label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
91	
92	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
93	\label{sec:daf}
94	
95	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:
96	\begin{equation}
97	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
98	\end{equation}
99	The approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
100	
101	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
102	\begin{equation}
103	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
104	\end{equation}%
105	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1, indicating death avoidance overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
106	
107	DAF is well-defined when $\Delta_{\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$---a mathematical consequence, not an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction.
108	
109	To assess sensitivity to penalty magnitude, we recalculate DAF under hypothetical death penalties (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}): $P{=}10 \rightarrow$ DAF $= 46.4\%$, $P{=}50 \rightarrow 81.2\%$, $P{=}100 \rightarrow 89.6\%$, $P{=}200 \rightarrow 94.5\%$. Death avoidance remains the dominant component across all penalty magnitudes, though its relative contribution scales with penalty severity. As a penalty-independent companion metric, the large majority of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\S\ref{sec:labeling}), confirming that death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.
```

As a penalty-independent companion metric, the large majority of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:labeling}), confirming that death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the exact text. Let me plan and execute all edits systematically.

**CHANGES NEEDED:**

### 1. Abstract (abstract.tex)
a. MF3 scope qualifier: "Within completable-horizon tasks (11 of 30 ScienceWorld task types" → "Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types"
b. 78.9% + DAF restructure: "roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry alone---a penalty-independent measure. The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor reaches ${\sim}90\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, with per-task correlation $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) between death-rate differential and performance gap;

we note that this correlation is partly structural, as death penalties contribute directly to score differences (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp})." → "78.9\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry---a penalty-independent metric. Under the default penalty $P{=}100$, the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) reaches ${\sim}90\%$, though DAF varies from 46\% to 95\% across penalty settings (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Per-task death rate correlates strongly with routing signal ($r{=}0.97$, though partly structural as death penalties contribute directly to score differences; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp})."

### 2. Introduction (introduction.tex)
a. Line...

Updating specific percentages and metrics across multiple sections of the paper---replacing vague language like "roughly four-fifths" and "the large majority" with the precise 78.9% figure (75 of 95 labels) throughout the introduction, conclusion, and methods sections.

Now I'm looking at the experiments and discussion sections to apply similar updates, and I need to reconsider how the abstract frames the core contribution before making these final changes. understanding what drives the value of routing decisions remains unclear. We propose episode-level counterfactual analysis: running complete episodes under pure strategies and comparing final outcomes to decompose routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing (245 episodes from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry alone---a penalty-independent measure. The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor reaches ~90% at the default penalty, with per-task correlation r = 0.97 (p < 10^-6) between death-rate differential and performance gap; this correlation is partly structural, as death penalties contribute directly to score differences. A trained router achieves only 57--59% balanced accuracy due to extreme class imbalance driven by death dominance. Cross-environment analysis on MATH shows routing signal vanishes without a death penalty: the label distribution inverts from 86:9 (deterministic-preferred) to 30:17 (internal directionally favored), with a marginally significant reversal of +4.3 percentage points (p = 0.079). A Llama-3.1-8B pilot provides preliminary evidence that death-driven routing generalizes across model families. These findings suggest that in environments with catastrophic failure modes, routing value is dominated by safety---the router functions primarily as a safety critic rather than an efficiency optimizer. The per-task death rate shows a strong correlation with routing signal, though this relationship is partly structural since death penalties directly influence score differences. Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes across completable-horizon tasks with a Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone, this analysis shows that roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels stem from death asymmetry alone—a measure independent of penalty settings.

For the Discussion section, I'm revising the negative result framing: the trained router's near-chance balanced accuracy (57.6%, with cross-split variance of 55.4±5.2%) illustrates how death asymmetry's extreme class imbalance (86:9 detection-to-intervention ratio) starves the minority class of sufficient signal for supervised learning to leverage, creating a structural constraint on router performance.
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "4051b957-aae7-4ae5-81de-cf5b32755d73",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "2baa84c5-7f70-4e1f-9f66-093efc9c590f",

  "uuid": "b67c1056-2161-44a1-a0e3-4ce266a3196f",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Across 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; roughly four-fifths of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.",
    "newString": "Among task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 78.9\\% of non-tie labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for ${\\sim}$90\\% of the routing signal} (at default penalty $P{=}100$) in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAcross 11 ScienceWorld task types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; roughly four-fifths of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is ${\\sim}90\\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\\% ($P{=}200$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a marginally significant reversal ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$): internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.\nAs a downstream consequence, a trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only ${\\sim}58\\%$ (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude. The penalty-sensitive DAF reaches ${\\sim}90\\%$ at the default $P{=}100$ but is lower at milder penalties (46\\% at $P{=}10$), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-context validation.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), providing evidence that the finding is driven by failure structure rather than an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of the paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.\n\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.\n\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}--\\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

The penalty-dependent DAF metric reaches ${\\sim}90\\%$ at $P{=}100$ but varies substantially across penalty settings (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}---a text-based science simulation with 30 task types---faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources---accuracy, latency, capability matching---and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\n\nWe find a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{death avoidance accounts for ${\\sim}$90\\% of the routing signal} (at default penalty $P{=}100$) in ScienceWorld.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies nearly vanishes.\n\nReaching this conclusion requires a methodological contribution.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels---per-step oracle comparison---degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nWe instead propose \\emph{episode-level counterfactual analysis}: run complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compare episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\n\nAmong task types exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening (11 of 30 available ScienceWorld task types, 245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.\nExcluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 78.9\\% of non-tie labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent measure.\nThe penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \\S\\ref{sec:daf}) is ${\\sim}90\\%$ at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 46\\% ($P{=}10$) to 95\\% ($P{=}200$).\nThe per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$), confirming that death avoidance is the dominant mechanism across task types (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation (300 problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, shows a marginally significant reversal ($+4.3$~pp, $p = 0.079$): internal reasoning outperforms code interpreter---the opposite direction from ScienceWorld.\nIn the absence of death risk, the deterministic advantage vanishes.\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($N{=}10$ episode pairs, 2 task types) shows directionally consistent death patterns within ScienceWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), suggesting the phenomenon is not specific to a single model, though full-scale multi-model validation remains future work.\nAs a downstream consequence, a trained router (Qwen3-1.7B with LoRA) achieves balanced accuracy of only ${\\sim}58\\%$ (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Routing signal decomposition.}\n    We identify step-level oracle degeneracy in sparse-reward environments and introduce episode-level counterfactual analysis with the DAF metric, a methodology for quantifying what fraction of routing signal derives from catastrophic-failure avoidance (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Death avoidance dominance.}\n    On ScienceWorld, death asymmetry drives roughly four-fifths of non-tie routing labels independent of penalty magnitude. The penalty-sensitive DAF reaches ${\\sim}90\\%$ at the default $P{=}100$ but is lower at milder penalties (46\\% at $P{=}10$), and a trained router does not significantly outperform always-deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-context validation.}\n    On MATH ($N{=}300$), where death risk is absent, the routing signal reverses direction (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), providing evidence that the finding is driven by failure structure rather than an inherent advantage of deterministic computation.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of the paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes the counterfactual collection procedure, episode-level labeling, and the DAF metric.\n\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp} presents the death avoidance decomposition and correlation analysis.\n\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}--\\ref{sec:cross-model} report baselines, router diagnostics, and cross-model verification.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses cross-environment evidence and implications.\n",
